Appendix A
Proof of Eq. (
19
)

Recall
A
is an isometry such that
A^{\dagger}A=\mathbbm{1}
and
AA^{\dagger}=\mathcal{P}
.
As a result, we have the following identity:

\displaystyle\norm{\ket{\Psi} - A\ket{\Phi}}-A\ket{\Phi}^{2}=

\displaystyle\norm{\ket{\Psi} - AA^\dag\ket{\Psi}}-AA^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}^{2}

\displaystyle+A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi} - \ket{\Phi}}-\ket{\Phi}^{2}.

(32)

This identity introduces the unnormalized intermediate states
A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}
and invites an interpretation of the MM error as the sum of a projection error and an MPS truncation error, which are respectively the first term and the second term in Eq. (
A
).

As we prove below, for a uniform finite system of size
L
, to the first order in the errors, both errors are proportional to
L
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\norm{\ket{\Psi} - AA^\dag\ket{\Psi}}-AA^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}^{2}\approx[1-\lambda_{1}(T_{A^{\dagger}\Psi:A^{\dagger}\Psi})]L\\
A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi} - \ket{\Phi}}-\ket{\Phi}^{2}\approx[1-\left(\lambda_{1}(T_{\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\Psi}:\Phi})\right)^{2}]L,\end{split}

(33)

where
\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}\propto A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}
is the normalized state
9
9

9

Note that it is wrong to use
T_{A^{\dagger}\Psi:\Lambda}
in Eq. (
33
) as the states making up the transfer matrix are not properly normalized
.
Recall the definitions as in Eq. (
20
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{p}

\displaystyle\equiv 1-\lambda_{1}(T_{A^{\dagger}\Psi:A^{\dagger}\Psi}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{t}

\displaystyle\equiv 1-\left(\lambda_{1}(T_{\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\Psi}:\Phi})\right)^{2}.

Evidently, the total error density in Eq. (
17
) can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{t}+O(\varepsilon_{p}^{2},\varepsilon_{t}^{2},\varepsilon_{p}\varepsilon_{t}),

(34)

which is Eq. (
19
).

To complete this appendix, we now give the derivation of Eq. (
33
).
The first term in Eq. (
A
) is due to the projection
AA^{\dagger}
:

\displaystyle\norm{\ket{\Psi}-AA^\dag\ket{\Psi}}-AA^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}^{2}

\displaystyle=1-A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi}}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv 1-(1-\varepsilon_{p})^{L}\approx\varepsilon_{p}L.

(35)

The second source of
\varepsilon
comes from the truncation error of representing the unnormalized state
A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}
with the MPS
\ket{\Phi}
:

\begin{split}&A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi} - \ket{\Phi}}-\ket{\Phi}^{2}=1+A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi}}^{2}-2\text{Re}\braket{\Phi|A^{\dagger}|\Psi}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=2-\varepsilon_{p}L-2\text{Re}\bra{\Phi}\frac{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}{\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon_{p}}\right)^{L}}\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon_{p}}\right)^{L}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\equiv 2-\varepsilon_{p}L-2\text{Re}\bra{\Phi}\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon_{p}})^{L},\end{split}

(36)

where we defined
\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}=A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}/{\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon_{p}}\right)^{L}}
as the normalized state.
Analogously to Eq. (
18
),

\norm{\ket{\Phi} - \widetilde{A^\dag\ket{\Psi}}}-\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}^{2}=2-2\text{Re}\bra{\Phi}\widetilde{A^{\dagger}\ket{\Psi}}\approx\varepsilon_{t}L.

(37)

Thus, putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle A^{\dagger}\norm{A^\dag\ket{\Psi} - \ket{\Phi}}-\ket{\Phi}^{2}

\displaystyle\approx 2-\varepsilon_{p}L-(2-\varepsilon_{t}L)\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{p}L}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{t}L.

(38)

{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-15.60466pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.98 0 C -3.51 0.28 -1.11 1.11 0.55 2.08 L 0.55 -2.08 C -1.11 -1.11 -3.51 -0.28 -4.98 0}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(39)

To enlarge the bond dimension
\chi_{v}=\dim(i_{2})=\dim(i_{3})
to
\chi^{\prime}_{v}
while keeping the operator that
A
represents invariant and the isometric condition intact, one groups
i_{2}i_{0}
and
i_{3}i_{1}
respectively as the row and column index of the isometric matrix
a
.

i_{2}
and
i_{3}
are the “slow” index of their respective combined indices.
Here we assume
\dim(i_{0})=\dim(i_{1})
, and thus
a
is square.
To enlarge the bond dimensions, one first zero-pads on the index
i_{2}
and then adds orthogonal columns on index
i_{3}
:

[a]\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}a\\
0\end{bmatrix}\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{\bot}\end{bmatrix},

(40)

where
a^{\bot}
is an arbitrary unitary matrix with
(\chi^{\prime}_{v}-\chi_{v})\dim(i_{0})
number of rows and columns.
Thus, when
\dim(i_{0})=\dim(i_{1})
, the result of isometric filling is
a\rightarrow a^{\prime}=a\oplus a^{\bot}
.

Appendix C
Area law in Pealing iMPS

As discussed in Sec.
IV.2
, iMM has a disentangling effect on the new iMPS
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
and we have shown by computing the half-chain entropy of the iMPS
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
over the peeling process:

\displaystyle S(\ket{\Phi_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\log\sigma_{i}^{2},

where
\sigma_{i}
are the singular values on a vertical bond of
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
; see Fig.
10
(a).
This entropy is for the half-chain subsystem composed of the physical indices and the virtual indices of
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
and is thus not physical.

To dig deeper, we ask whether iMM can extract
\alpha(g)
using only physical quantities
10
10

10

Here by physical quantities, we mean any quantity obtainable in principle from the initial wavefunction
\ket{\Phi_{0}}
.
The answer is yes.
For each
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
, a further iMM can be applied to
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
with
\chi_{h}=1
:

\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}\approx A^{\prime}_{\ell}\ket{\Phi_{\ell}^{\prime}},

(41)

where the isoTNO
A_{\ell}^{\prime}
has no physical legs.
The original
\ket{\Phi_{l}}
has virtual bonds and can be viewed as the purification state of the density matrix over the remaining physical spins.
Now, the
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}^{\prime}}
is a pure state and the dominant eigenstate of such a density matrix.
Remarkably, we find that
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}^{\prime}}
has an error density on the order of
10^{-5}
when compared to the iMPS representing width
L_{x}-\ell
TFI ground state and thus has an
S(\ket{\Phi^{\prime}_{\ell}})
that obeys the entropy area law almost perfectly while also agreeing with the values found via iDMRG (see blue crosses Fig.
10
(b)).
This suggests that the TFI Hamiltonian of width
L
and the entanglement Hamiltonian of a subsystem of width
L
in a larger TFI system have approximately the same ground state.
We repeat this experiment at the critical transverse field
g=g_{C}^{2D}
and find the same behavior. Results are shown in Appendix
D
.

Area law properties of peeled isoTNS.

Appendix D
Numerical results for
g=3.04438

Here we present numerics at
g=g_{C}^{2D}=3.04438
, the critical transverse field for the two-dimensional TFI in the thermodynamic limit.
Close to the critical point, we expect this model on finite width strips to be more difficult to capture by an iMPS due to increased entanglement.

First we repeat the area law experiment of Sec.
V.2
and show the results in Fig.
11
.
We again see that the iMM algorithm removes an amount of entanglement
\alpha(g)
per column consistent with the area law.
Additionally, the physical entropy
S(\ket{\Phi_{\ell}^{\prime}})
agrees with the entropy from iDMRG, again indicating that the orthogonality column of
L_{x}-\ell
columns and the iMPS of the same width have large overlap.
We note that for
g=3.50
, the amount of entanglement removed per column saturates to
\alpha(g)
sooner than in the
g=304438
case, indicating that more horizontal entropy is present in latter case.

Figure 11
:
Entropy of
\ket{\Phi_{\ell}}
as a function of number of columns
\ell
removed by peeling the ground state of the 2D TFI Hamiltonian with width
L_{x}=8
and
g=3.04438
.
After an initial delay, explained by
S(\ket{\Phi_{\ell}})
not being a physical entropy, iMM removes an amount of entanglement consistent with the area law.
Physical entropy
S(\ket{\Phi_{\ell}^{\prime}})
(as defined in Fig.
10
(a)) and entropy from iDMRG GS of different width strips agree and obey area law.

Next we use the iTEBD
2
algorithm to search for the ground state of the
g=3.04438
2D TFI.
Again we compare isoTNS energies evaluated by iMM against iDMRG energies for
\chi=512
.
Results for
L_{x}=4
,
L_{x}=8
, and
L_{x}=20
infinite strips are shown in Fig.
12
, where again we find an intermediate
\mathrm{d}t
, which balances iTEBD and iMM errors, leads to the optimal energies.

Figure 12
:
Ground state energies achieved with iTEBD
2
for critical
g=3.04438
2D TFI on
L_{x}=4,8,20
strip.
An intermediate
\mathrm{d}\tau
and
f
yield the best energy, while
\chi=8
outperforms
\chi=4
.
We compare the isoTNS energies against essentially exact energies from quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) extrapolated from strips of finite length.
As a comparison, the dashed line is the result of an iDMRG calculation with bond dimension
\chi=512
. For
L_{x}=4,8
, the iDMRG result is below the bottom axis of the plot.
